{"nodes": [{"id": "bd4e66c0-851a-45f1-8dc0-cd99cc2b3df0", "label": "verify dim", "group": "personal", "content": "verify dim", "sector": "semantic", "salience": 1.0}, {"id": "22e9da00-2413-4eaf-9f7d-c9e66ef67051", "label": "#skill - SKILL: design\\ability-spectrum...", "group": "personal", "content": "SKILL: design\\ability-spectrum-mapping\\ability_spectrum_mapping_skill.md\n\n---\nname: ability-spectrum-mapping\ndescription: \"Map how a product or feature works across the full spectrum of human ability ÔÇö not just 'can use' and 'can't use'. Use when planning accessibility coverage, identifying gaps, or communicating accessibility scope to stakeholders. Triggers on: ability spectrum, range of users, who can use this, who can't use this, accessibility coverage, accessibility scope, ability range, spectrum of needs.\"\n---\n\n# Ability Spectrum Mapping\n\nMap how a feature performs across the full range of human ability ÔÇö\nfrom full capability to no capability in each dimension ÔÇö to identify\nwhere the design starts to break down and who gets excluded.\n\n## Why a Spectrum\n\nAccessibility is not binary. It's not \"can see\" vs \"blind.\" The\nreality is a continuous spectrum:\n\n- Full vision ÔåÆ needs glasses ÔåÆ low vision ÔåÆ legally blind ÔåÆ no vision\n- Full hearing ÔåÆ mild loss ÔåÆ moderate loss ÔåÆ severe loss ÔåÆ deaf\n- Full dexterity ÔåÆ mild tremor ÔåÆ limited range ÔåÆ single hand ÔåÆ no hands\n- Full cognition ÔåÆ mild difficulty ÔåÆ moderate difficulty ÔåÆ significant support needs\n\nProducts don't go from \"works\" to \"doesn't work\" in a single step.\nThey degrade gradually. The spectrum map shows you exactly where your\ndesign starts failing and for whom.\n\n## How to Build an Ability Spectrum Map\n\n### Step 1: List Core Tasks\nIdentify the 5ÔÇô10 most important tasks a user performs with the product.\n\n### Step 2: Define the Spectrums\nFor each relevant ability dimension, define 4ÔÇô5 points:\n\n**Vision spectrum:**\nFull vision ÔåÆ Corrected vision ÔåÆ Low vision (magnification) ÔåÆ\nVery low vision (screen reader + some vision) ÔåÆ No vision (screen reader only)\n\n**Hearing spectrum:**\nFull hearing ÔåÆ Mild loss (louder volume) ÔåÆ Moderate loss (captions needed) ÔåÆ\nSevere loss (captions + visual alerts) ÔåÆ Deaf (fully visual communication)\n\n**Motor spectrum:**\nFull dexterity ÔåÆ Reduced precision (larger targets) ÔåÆ Limited range\n(keyboard only) ÔåÆ Minimal movement (switch or voice) ÔåÆ\nNo hand use (eye tracking, sip-and-puff)\n\n**Cognitive spectrum:**\nComfortable with complexity ÔåÆ Prefers simplicity ÔåÆ Needs plain language ÔåÆ\nNeeds step-by-step guidance ÔåÆ Needs significant support\n\n### Step 3: Map Each Task\n\nFor each task ├ù ability point, rate:\n- **Works well** ÔÇö no barriers\n- **Works with friction** ÔÇö possible but harder than it should be\n- **Works with workaround** ÔÇö user must find their own solution\n- **Fails** ÔÇö task cannot be completed\n\n### Step 4: Identify the Breakpoints\n\nWhere does the design shift from \"works\" to \"fails\"? That breakpoint\nis your accessibility boundary. Everything to the right of it is\nexcluded.\n\nThe goal is to push that boundary as far right as possible.\n\n## Presenting to Stakeholders\n\nThe spectrum map is a powerful communication tool because it shows:\n- Exactly how many users are affected at each point\n- Where investment in accessibility has the highest return\n- That accessibility is a continuum, not a checkbox\n- Which fixes would move the boundary and include more people\n\n## Template\n\n| Task | Full vision | Low vision | Screen reader |\n|------|------------|-----------|---------------|\n| Search for a product | Works well | Works with friction (small targets) | Fails (search suggestions not announced) |\n| Complete checkout | Works well | Works well | Works with friction (form errors not associated) |\n\n## Assessment Questions\n\n1. Have you mapped core tasks across at least vision, motor, and\n   cognitive spectrums?\n2. Can you identify the exact breakpoint where each task fails?\n3. Do you know which fixes would include the most additional users?\n4. Can you communicate the spectrum to stakeholders without\n   technical jargon?\n", "sector": "procedural", "salience": 1.0}, {"id": "3eef022a-272a-4ae1-a11a-ca133427d9f6", "label": "#skill - SKILL: design\\accessibility-de...", "group": "personal", "content": "SKILL: design\\accessibility-debt-tracking\\accessibility_debt_tracking_skill.md\n\n---\nname: accessibility-debt-tracking\ndescription: \"Track and manage accessibility debt ÔÇö known accessibility issues that have been deferred. Use when managing a backlog of accessibility issues, planning remediation, or when accessibility problems are accumulating faster than they're being fixed. Triggers on: accessibility debt, tech debt, known issues, backlog, deferred, we'll fix it later, remediation, accessibility backlog, accumulating issues, regression.\"\n---\n\n# Accessibility Debt Tracking\n\nTrack accessibility debt the way you'd track technical debt ÔÇö as a\nknown liability with a cost that compounds over time.\n\n## What Is Accessibility Debt\n\nAccessibility debt is every known accessibility issue that exists\nin your product but hasn't been fixed. It includes:\n- Issues found in audits that were deprioritised\n- Tradeoffs where accessibility was deferred\n- New features shipped without full accessibility\n- Regressions introduced by updates or redesigns\n- Known gaps in assistive technology support\n\n## Why It Compounds\n\nUnlike a feature request that just waits, accessibility debt\ngrows:\n- Each new feature built on top of an inaccessible foundation\n  inherits the inaccessibility\n- Users with disabilities develop workarounds that break when\n  you eventually fix things differently than they expect\n- Legal exposure increases with time and awareness\n- The longer an issue persists, the more expensive it becomes\n  to fix (code has grown around it)\n- Team knowledge of why the issue exists fades\n\n## Debt Record Format\n\nFor each known accessibility issue, track:\n\n**Issue:** clear description of the accessibility barrier\n**Severity:** critical (blocks task) / major (significant difficulty) /\nminor (friction)\n**Who's affected:** specific user groups\n**Where:** page, component, or flow\n**WCAG criteria:** which success criteria it violates\n**Discovered:** date and how (audit, user report, testing)\n**Deferred because:** the reason it wasn't fixed immediately\n**Workaround:** any temporary alternative for affected users\n**Estimated fix effort:** small / medium / large\n**Remediation deadline:** when it must be fixed\n**Owner:** who is responsible for the fix\n\n## Managing the Debt\n\n### Triage Rules\n- **Critical (blocks task):** fix in current sprint, no exceptions\n- **Major (significant difficulty):** fix within 30 days\n- **Minor (friction):** fix within 90 days\n- Any issue older than its deadline gets escalated\n\n### Preventing New Debt\n- Accessibility acceptance criteria on every story\n- Accessibility check before merge (automated + manual)\n- Regression testing for previously fixed issues\n- New features cannot ship with critical accessibility debt\n\n### Paying Down Debt\n- Allocate a percentage of each sprint to debt reduction\n  (10ÔÇô20% is common)\n- Fix debt when you're already touching the component\n- Prioritise by: severity ├ù number of users affected\n- Celebrate debt reduction ÔÇö make it visible\n\n### Tracking Regressions\n- When a previously fixed issue reappears, it's a regression\n- Regressions should be treated as critical by default\n- Add automated tests for every fix to prevent recurrence\n- Track regression rate as a quality metric\n\n## Assessment Questions\n\n1. Is there a documented list of known accessibility issues?\n2. Does each issue have a severity, owner, and deadline?\n3. Is accessibility debt being paid down each sprint?\n4. Are regressions caught and treated as critical?\n5. Is the debt trend improving or worsening over time?\n", "sector": "procedural", "salience": 1.0}, {"id": "3edd4813-57ff-45a3-83da-b32e0f17efb8", "label": "#skill - SKILL: design\\accessibility-te...", "group": "personal", "content": "SKILL: design\\accessibility-testing-strategy\\accessibility_testing_strategy_skill.md\n\n---\nname: accessibility-testing-strategy\ndescription: \"Plan what to test, how to test, and who should test for accessibility. Use when defining a testing approach, planning QA, setting up automated and manual testing, or deciding what level of accessibility testing a project needs. Triggers on: accessibility testing, how to test, test plan, automated testing, manual testing, screen reader testing, user testing, accessibility QA, what to test, testing strategy.\"\n---\n\n# Accessibility Testing Strategy\n\nPlan a testing approach that catches real accessibility barriers ÔÇö\nbecause automated tools find roughly 30% of issues. The other 70%\nrequire human judgment.\n\n## The Testing Pyramid\n\n### Layer 1: Automated Testing (Catches ~30%)\nWhat it finds:\n- Missing alt text\n- Colour contrast failures\n- Missing form labels\n- Missing language attribute\n- Empty headings and buttons\n- Duplicate IDs\n\nWhat it misses:\n- Whether alt text is meaningful\n- Whether focus order is logical\n- Whether content makes sense\n- Whether interactions work with keyboard\n- Whether the experience is usable\n\nTools: axe, Lighthouse, WAVE, Pa11y, jest-axe\nWhen: on every build, in CI/CD pipeline\nWho: automated ÔÇö no human needed\n\n### Layer 2: Manual Expert Testing (Catches ~50%)\nWhat it finds:\n- Keyboard navigation issues\n- Focus management problems\n- Logical reading order failures\n- Meaningful alt text assessment\n- Cognitive accessibility barriers\n- Interaction pattern issues\n\nMethod:\n- Tab through every interactive element\n- Test with screen reader (VoiceOver or NVDA)\n- Test at 200% zoom\n- Test with system preferences enabled\n- Review content for plain language\n\nWhen: before each release, on major feature changes\nWho: someone trained in accessibility testing\n\n### Layer 3: User Testing With Disabled People (Catches remaining ~20%)\nWhat it finds:\n- Real-world usability barriers that experts miss\n- Assistive technology compatibility issues\n- Unexpected workflows and workarounds\n- Cognitive and emotional barriers\n- Issues that only appear with actual AT proficiency\n\nMethod:\n- Recruit participants with diverse disabilities\n- Test core tasks with their own devices and AT\n- Observe ÔÇö don't guide or explain\n- Record what works, what fails, and what frustrates\n\nWhen: quarterly, or before major launches\nWho: users with disabilities using their own setup\n\n## What to Test First\n\n### Priority 1: Core Tasks\n- Can users complete the primary tasks the product exists for?\n- Test the critical path end to end\n- Any failure here is a launch blocker\n\n### Priority 2: Input and Navigation\n- Keyboard-only navigation through the core flow\n- Screen reader navigation through the core flow\n- Touch interaction on mobile for the core flow\n\n### Priority 3: Content and Comprehension\n- Is content understandable at the target reading level?\n- Are error messages helpful?\n- Is help available and findable?\n\n### Priority 4: Edge Cases and Preferences\n- Zoom to 200%\n- System preferences (reduced motion, high contrast, dark mode)\n- Long content, empty states, error states\n\n## Screen Reader Testing Basics\n\nFor teams new to screen reader testing:\n\n### Start With VoiceOver (Mac)\n- Turn on: Cmd + F5\n- Navigate: VO + arrow keys (VO = Ctrl + Option)\n- Headings: VO + Cmd + H\n- Links: VO + Cmd + L\n- Forms: VO + Cmd + J\n- Turn off: Cmd + F5\n\n### Start With NVDA (Windows)\n- Download free from nvaccess.org\n- Navigate: Tab, arrow keys\n- Headings: H\n- Links: K\n- Forms: F\n- Stop speech: Ctrl\n\n### What to Listen For\n- Does every interactive element have a name?\n- Are state changes announced (expanded, selected, checked)?\n- Does focus move to the right place after actions?\n- Can you understand the page reading only the headings?\n\n## Assessment Questions\n\n1. Is automated testing running on every build?\n2. Is manual expert testing happening before each release?\n3. Has the product been tested by people with disabilities?\n4. Are core tasks tested with keyboard and screen reader?\n5. Is there a clear priority for what to test first?\n", "sector": "procedural", "salience": 1.0}, {"id": "459cafef-050e-4f9c-b571-510bc6df6296", "label": "#skill - SKILL: design\\accessible-conte...", "group": "personal", "content": "SKILL: design\\accessible-content-review\\accessible_content_review_skill.md\n\n---\nname: review\ndescription: \"Review content for accessibility across cognitive and literacy levels. Chains: readable-content, alt-text-design, heading-structure, link-text-design, table-accessibility, form-labelling, multimedia-accessibility. Use when auditing existing content for accessibility.\"\n---\n\n# Accessible Content Review\n\nRun a comprehensive review of content accessibility ÔÇö covering\nstructure, language, images, links, tables, forms, and multimedia.\n\n## Process\n\n### Phase 1: Structure Check\nUsing **heading-structure**, verify the document outline:\n- One H1, no skipped levels, headings describe content\n- Someone reading only headings would understand the page\n\n### Phase 2: Readability Check\nUsing **readable-content**, assess the text:\n- Reading level appropriate to audience\n- Sentences under 20 words, paragraphs under 4 sentences\n- Most important information first\n- Scannable with headings, bold, and lists\n\n### Phase 3: Image and Media Check\nUsing **alt-text-design** and **multimedia-accessibility**, review:\n- Every image has appropriate alt text (or empty alt for decorative)\n- Complex images have extended descriptions\n- Video has captions, audio has transcripts\n- Media players are keyboard accessible\n\n### Phase 4: Link and Navigation Check\nUsing **link-text-design**, verify:\n- All link text makes sense out of context\n- No generic \"click here\" or \"read more\"\n- File links state type and size\n\n### Phase 5: Table and Form Check\nUsing **table-accessibility** and **form-labelling**, review:\n- Tables have headers, captions, and responsive behaviour\n- Forms have visible labels, grouping, and error association\n- No placeholder-only labels\n\n## Output\n\nPresent findings as:\n\n1. **Summary** ÔÇö overall content accessibility rating\n2. **Issue list** ÔÇö each issue with location, type, severity, and fix\n3. **Quick wins** ÔÇö issues that can be fixed in under 5 minutes each\n4. **Structural issues** ÔÇö problems that need redesign or rewriting\n\nSeverity definitions:\n- **Critical** ÔÇö content is inaccessible to one or more user groups\n- **Major** ÔÇö content is difficult to access or understand\n- **Minor** ÔÇö "group": "personal", "content": "SKILL: design\\adaptive-personalisation\\adaptive_personalisation_skill.md\n\n---\nname: contextual-help-design\ndescription: \"Design help systems and support patterns that work for people with cognitive disabilities. Use when designing help content, tooltips, onboarding, FAQs, support flows, documentation, or any context where users need guidance. Triggers on: help, support, FAQ, tooltip, onboarding, tutorial, guidance, stuck, confused, documentation, instructions.\"\n---\n\n# Contextual Help Design\n\nDesign help that meets people where they are ÔÇö on the screen they're\nstuck on, in language they understand, without requiring them to\nremember how they got there.\n\n## Core Principle\n\nIf a user gets stuck, they should be able to get help without leaving\nthe screen they're on.\n\n## Design Patterns\n\n### Inline Help\n- Tooltip or info icon next to fields that need explanation\n- Examples shown next to input fields (\"e.g., 15/03/2026\")\n- Help text visible by default for complex fields\n- Inline definitions for specialist terms (underline + tooltip)\n- \"What's this?\" links for uncommon concepts\n\n### Guided Tasks\n- Step-by-step wizard as an alternative to complex forms\n- Examples of completed tasks (filled-in sample forms)\n- Templates or pre-built options alongside \"start from scratch\"\n- Progress-aware hints that change based on where the user is\n\n### Escalation Path\n- Self-service help (search, FAQ) as first option\n- Chat or messaging as second option\n- Phone or video as third option\n- Never require users to re-explain when escalating between channels\n- Show estimated wait times for human support\n- Never require a reference number to get help\n\n### Error Recovery Support\n- Error messages include: what happened + why + what to do next\n- Link directly to the field or step that needs fixing\n- Offer alternative paths when primary fails\n- For complex errors: offer human support directly\n\n## Help Content Rules\n\n- Write at grade 4ÔÇô6 reading level\n- Use the same terminology as the interface (not different words)\n- One instruction per step\n- Include images or diagrams for spatial or procedural instructions\n- Make help searchable\n- Test help content with people who have the actual problem\n\n## Assessment Questions\n\n1. levels)\n- [ ] All CRUD operations properly mapped to HTTP methods\n\n### HTTP Methods\n\n- [ ] GET for retrieval (safe, idempotent)\n- [ ] POST for creation\n- [ ] PUT for full replacement (idempotent)\n- [ ] PATCH for partial updates\n- [ ] DELETE for removal (idempotent)\n\n### Status Codes\n\n- [ ] 200 OK for successful GET/PATCH/PUT\n- [ ] 201 Created for POST\n- [ ] 204 No Content for DELETE\n- [ ] 400 Bad Request for malformed requests\n- [ ] 401 Unauthorized for missing auth\n- [ ] 403 Forbidden for insufficient permissions\n- [ ] 404 Not Found for missing resources\n- [ ] 422 Unprocessable Entity for validation errors\n- [ ] 429 Too Many Requests for rate limiting\n- [ ] 500 Internal Server Error for server issues\n\n### Pagination\n\n- [ ] All collection endpoints paginated\n- [ ] Default page size defined (e.g., 20)\n- [ ] Maximum page size enforced (e.g., 100)\n- [ ] Pagination metadata included (total, pages, etc.)\n- [ ] Cursor-based or offset-based pattern chosen\n\n### Filtering & Sorting\n\n- [ ] Query parameters for filtering\n- [ ] Sort parameter supported\n- [ ] Search parameter for full-text search\n- [ ] Field selection supported (sparse Limiting\n\n- [ ] Rate limits defined per endpoint/user\n- [ ] Rate limit headers included\n- [ ] 429 status code for exceeded limits\n- [ ] Retry-After header provided\n\n### Documentation\n\n- [ ] design\\api-design-princ...", "group": "personal", "content": "SKILL: design\\api-design-principles\\references\\graphql-schema-design.md\n\n# GraphQL Schema Design Patterns\n\n## Schema Organization\n\n### Modular Schema Structure\n\n```graphql\n# user.graphql\ntype User {\n  id: ID!\n  email: String!\n  name: String!\n  posts: [Post!]!\n}\n\nextend type Query {\n  user(id: ID!): User\n  users(first: Int, after: String): UserConnection!\n}\n\nextend type Mutation {\n  createUser(input: CreateUserInput!): CreateUserPayload!\n}\n\n# post.graphql\ntype Post {\n  id: ID!\n  title: String!\n  content: String!\n  author: User!\n}\n\nextend type Query {\n  post(id: ID!): Post\n}\n```\n\n## Type Design Patterns\n\n### 1. Non-Null Types\n\n```graphql\ntype User {\n  id: ID! # Always required\n  email: String! # Required\n  phone: String # Optional (nullable)\n  posts: [Post!]! # Non-null array of non-null posts\n  tags: [String!] # Nullable array of non-null strings\n}\n```\n\n### 2. Interfaces for Polymorphism\n\n```graphql\ninterface Node {\n  id: ID!\n  createdAt: DateTime!\n}\n\ntype User implements Node {\n  id: ID!\n  createdAt: DateTime!\n  email: String!\n}\n\ntype Post implements Node {\n  id: ID!\n  createdAt: DateTime!\n  title: String!\n}\n\ntype Query {\n  node(id: ID!): Node\n}\n```\n\n### 3. Unions for Heterogeneous Results\n\n```graphql\nunion SearchResult = User | Post | Comment\n\ntype Query {\n  search(query: String!): [SearchResult!]!\n}\n\n# Query example\n{\n  search(query: \"graphql\") {\n    ... on User {\n      name\n      email\n    }\n    ... on Post {\n      title\n      content\n    }\n    ... on Comment {\n      text\n      author {\n        name\n      }\n    }\n  }\n}\n```\n\n### 4. Input Types\n\n```graphql\ninput CreateUserInput {\n  email: String!\n  name: String!\n  password: String!\n  profileInput: ProfileInput\n}\n\ninput ProfileInput {\n  bio: String\n  avatar: String\n  website: String\n}\n\ninput UpdateUserInput {\n  id: ID!\n  email: String\n  name: String\n  profileInput: ProfileInput\n}\n```\n\n## Pagination Patterns\n\n### Relay Cursor Pagination (Recommended)\n\n```graphql\ntype UserConnection {\n  edges: [UserEdge!]!\n  pageInfo: PageInfo!\n  totalCount: Int!\n}\n\ntype UserEdge {\n  node: User!\n  cursor: String!\n}\n\ntype PageInfo {\n  hasNextPage: Boolean!\n  hasPreviousPage: Boolean!\n  startCursor: String\n  endCursor: String\n}\n\ntype Query {\n  users(first: Int, after: String, last: Int, before: String): UserConnection!\n}\n\n# Usage\n{\n  users(first: 10, after: \"cursor123\") {\n    edges {\n      cursor\n      node {\n        id\n        name\n      }\n    }\n    pageInfo {\n      hasNextPage\n      endCursor\n    }\n  }\n}\n```\n\n### Offset Pagination (Simpler)\n\n```graphql\ntype UserList {\n  items: [User!]!\n  total: Int!\n  page: Int!\n  pageSize: Int!\n}\n\ntype Query {\n  users(page: Int = 1, pageSize: Int = 20): UserList!\n}\n```\n\n## Mutation Design Patterns\n\n### 1. Input/Payload Pattern\n\n```graphql\ninput CreatePostInput {\n  title: String!\n  content: String!\n  tags: [String!]\n}\n\ntype CreatePostPayload {\n  post: Post\n  errors: [Error!]\n  success: Boolean!\n}\n\ntype Error {\n  field: String\n  message: String!\n  code: String!\n}\n\ntype Mutation {\n  createPost(input: CreatePostInput!): CreatePostPayload!\n}\n```\n\n### 2. Optimistic Response Support\n\n```graphql\ntype UpdateUserPayload {\n  user: User\n  clientMutationId: String\n  errors: [Error!]\n}\n\ninput UpdateUserInput {\n  id: ID!\n  name: String\n  clientMutationId: String\n}\n\ntype Mutation {\n  updateUser(input: UpdateUserInput!): UpdateUserPayload!\n}\n```\n\n### 3. Batch Mutations\n\n```graphql\ninput BatchCreateUserInput {\n  users: [CreateUserInput!]!\n}\n\ntype BatchCreateUserPayload {\n  results: [CreateUserResult!]!\n  successCount: Int!\n  errorCount: Int!\n}\n\ntype CreateUserResult {\n  user: User\n  errors: [Error!]\n  index: Int!\n}\n\ntype Mutation {\n  batchCreateUsers(input: BatchCreateUserInput!): BatchCreateUserPayload!\n}\n```\n\n## Field Design\n\n### Arguments and Filtering\n\n```graphql\ntype Query {\n  posts(\n    # Pagination\n    first: Int = 20\n    after: String\n\n    # Filtering\n    status: PostStatus\n    authorId: ID\n    tag: String\n\n    # Sorting\n    orderBy: PostOrderBy = CREATED_AT\n    orderDirection: OrderDirection = DESC\n\n    # Searching\n    search: String\n  ): PostConnection!\n}\n\nenum PostStatus {\n  DRAFT\n  PUBLISHED\n  ARCHIVED\n}\n\nenum PostOrderBy {\n  CREATED_AT\n  UPDATED_AT\n  TITLE\n}\n\nenum OrderDirection {\n  ASC\n  DESC\n}\n```\n\n### Computed Fields\n\n```graphql\ntype User {\n  firstName: String!\n  lastName: String!\n  fullName: String! # Computed in resolver\n  posts: [Post!]!\n  postCount: Int! # Computed, doesn't load all posts\n}\n\ntype Post {\n  likeCount: Int!\n  commentCount: Int!\n  isLikedByViewer: Boolean! # Context-dependent\n}\n```\n\n## Subscriptions\n\n```graphql\ntype Subscription {\n  postAdded: Post!\n\n  postUpdated(postId: ID!): Post!\n\n  userStatusChanged(userId: ID!): UserStatus!\n}\n\ntype UserStatus {\n  userId: ID!\n  online: Boolean!\n  lastSeen: DateTime!\n}\n\n# Client usage\nsubscription {\n  postAdded {\n    id\n    title\n    author {\n      name\n    }\n  }\n}\n```\n\n## Custom Scalars\n\n```graphql\nscalar DateTime\nscalar Email\nscalar URL\nscalar JSON\nscalar Money\n\ntype User {\n  email: Email!\n  website: URL\n  createdAt: DateTime!\n  metadata: JSON\n}\n\ntype Product {\n  price: Money!\n}\n```\n\n## Directives\n\n### Built-in Directives\n\n```graphql\ntype User {\n  name: String!\n  email: String! @deprecated(reason: \"Use emails field instead\")\n  emails: [String!]!\n\n  # Conditional inclusion\n  privateData: PrivateData @include(if: $isOwner)\n}\n\n# Query\nquery GetUser($isOwner: Boolean!) {\n  user(id: \"123\") {\n    name\n    privateData @include(if: $isOwner) {\n      ssn\n    }\n  }\n}\n```\n\n### Custom Directives\n\n```graphql\ndirective @auth(requires: Role = USER) on FIELD_DEFINITION\n\nenum Role {\n  USER\n  ADMIN\n  MODERATOR\n}\n\ntype Mutation {\n  deleteUser(id: ID!): Boolean! @auth(requires: ADMIN)\n  updateProfile(input: ProfileInput!): User! @auth\n}\n```\n\n## Error Handling\n\n### Union Error Pattern\n\n```graphql\ntype User {\n  id: ID!\n  email: String!\n}\n\ntype ValidationError {\n  field: String!\n  message: String!\n}\n\ntype NotFoundError {\n  message: String!\n  resourceType: String!\n  resourceId: ID!\n}\n\ntype AuthorizationError {\n  message: String!\n}\n\nunion UserResult = User | ValidationError | NotFoundError | AuthorizationError\n\ntype Query {\n  user(id: ID!): UserResult!\n}\n\n# Usage\n{\n  user(id: \"123\") {\n    ... on User {\n      id\n      email\n    }\n    ... on NotFoundError {\n      message\n      resourceType\n    }\n    ... on AuthorizationError {\n      message\n    }\n  }\n}\n```\n\n### Errors in Payload\n\n```graphql\ntype CreateUserPayload {\n  user: User\n  errors: [Error!]\n  success: Boolean!\n}\n\ntype Error {\n  field: String\n  message: String!\n  code: ErrorCode!\n}\n\nenum ErrorCode {\n  VALIDATION_ERROR\n  UNAUTHORIZED\n  NOT_FOUND\n  INTERNAL_ERROR\n}\n```\n\n## N+1 Query Problem Solutions\n\n### DataLoader Pattern\n\n```python\nfrom aiodataloader import DataLoader\n\nclass PostLoader(DataLoader):\n    async def batch_load_fn(self, post_ids):\n        posts = await db.posts.find({\"id\": {\"$in\": post_ids}})\n        post_map = {post[\"id\"]: post for post in posts}\n        return [post_map.get(pid) for pid in post_ids]\n\n# Resolver\n@user_type.field(\"posts\")\nasync def resolve_posts(user, info):\n    loader = info.context[\"loaders\"][\"post\"]\n    return await loader.load_many(user[\"post_ids\"])\n```\n\n### Query Depth Limiting\n\n```python\nfrom graphql import GraphQLError\n\ndef depth_limit_validator(max_depth: int):\n    def validate(context, node, ancestors):\n        depth = len(ancestors)\n        if depth > max_depth:\n            raise GraphQLError(\n                f\"Query depth {depth} exceeds maximum {max_depth}\"\n            )\n    return validate\n```\n\n### Query Complexity Analysis\n\n```python\ndef complexity_limit_validator(max_complexity: int):\n    def calculate_complexity(node):\n        # Each field = 1, lists multiply\n        complexity = 1\n        if is_list_field(node):\n            complexity *= get_list_size_arg(node)\n        return complexity\n\n    return validate_complexity\n```\n\n## Schema Versioning\n\n### Field Deprecation\n\n```graphql\ntype User {\n  name: String! @deprecated(reason: \"Use firstName and lastName\")\n  firstName: String!\n  lastName: String!\n}\n```\n\n### Schema Evolution\n\n```graphql\n# v1 - Initial\ntype User {\n  name: String!\n}\n\n# v2 - Add optional field (backward compatible)\ntype User {\n  name: String!\n  email: String\n}\n\n# v3 - Deprecate and add new field\ntype User {\n  name: String! @deprecated(reason: \"Use firstName/lastName\")\n  firstName: String!\n  lastName: String!\n  email: String\n}\n```\n\n## Best Practices Summary\n\n1. **Nullable vs Non-Null**: Start nullable, make non-null when guaranteed\n2. **Input Types**: Always use input types for mutations\n3. **Payload Pattern**: Return errors in mutation payloads\n4. **Pagination**: Use cursor-based for infinite scroll, offset for simple cases\n5. **Naming**: Use camelCase for fields, PascalCase for types\n6. **Deprecation**: Use `@deprecated` instead of removing fields\n7. **DataLoaders**: Always use for relationships to prevent N+1\n8. **Complexity Limits**: Protect against expensive queries\n9. **Custom Scalars**: Use for domain-specific types (Email, DateTime)\n10. **Documentation**: accessibility-debt-tracking\ndescription: \"Track and manage accessibility debt ÔÇö known accessibility issues that have been deferred. Use when managing a backlog of accessibility issues, planning remediation, or when accessibility problems are accumulating faster than they're being fixed. Triggers on: accessibility debt, tech debt, known issues, backlog, deferred, we'll fix it later, remediation, accessibility backlog, accumulating issues, regression.\"\n---\n\n# Accessibility Debt Tracking\n\nTrack accessibility debt the way you'd track technical debt ÔÇö as a\nknown liability with a cost that compounds over time.\n\n## What Is Accessibility Debt\n\nAccessibility debt is every known accessibility issue that exists\nin your product but hasn't been fixed. It includes:\n- Issues found in audits that were deprioritised\n- Tradeoffs where accessibility was deferred\n- New features shipped without full accessibility\n- Regressions introduced by updates or redesigns\n- Known gaps in assistive technology support\n\n## Why It Compounds\n\nUnlike a feature request that just waits, accessibility debt\ngrows:\n- Each new feature built on top of an inaccessible foundation\n  inherits the inaccessibility\n- Users with disabilities develop workarounds that break when\n  you eventually fix things differently than they expect\n- Legal exposure increases with time and awareness\n- The longer an issue persists, the more expensive it becomes\n  to fix (code has grown around it)\n- Team knowledge of why the issue exists fades\n\n## Debt Record Format\n\nFor each known accessibility issue, track:\n\n**Issue:** clear description of the accessibility barrier\n**Severity:** critical (blocks task) / major (significant difficulty) /\nminor (friction)\n**Who's affected:** specific user groups\n**Where:** page, component, or flow\n**WCAG criteria:** which success criteria it violates\n**Discovered:** date and how (audit, user report, testing)\n**Deferred because:** the reason it wasn't fixed immediately\n**Workaround:** any temporary alternative for affected users\n**Estimated fix effort:** small / medium / large\n**Remediation deadline:** when it must be fixed\n**Owner:** who is responsible for the fix\n\n## Managing the Debt\n\n### Triage Rules\n- **Critical (blocks task):** fix in current sprint, no exceptions\n- **Major (significant difficulty):** fix within 30 days\n- **Minor (friction):** fix within 90 days\n- Any issue older than its deadline gets escalated\n\n### Preventing New Debt\n- Accessibility acceptance criteria on every story\n- Accessibility check before merge (automated + manual)\n- Regression testing for previously fixed issues\n- New features cannot ship with critical accessibility debt\n\n### Paying Down Debt\n- Allocate a percentage of each sprint to debt reduction\n  (10ÔÇô20% is common)\n- Fix debt when you're already touching the component\n- Prioritise by: severity ├ù number of users affected\n- Celebrate debt reduction ÔÇö make it visible\n\n### Tracking Regressions\n- When a previously fixed issue reappears, it's a regression\n- Regressions should be treated as critical by default\n- Add automated tests for every fix to prevent recurrence\n- Track regression rate as a quality metric\n\n## Assessment Questions\n\n1. Is there a documented list of known accessibility issues?\n2. Does each issue have a severity, owner, and deadline?\n3. Is accessibility debt being paid down each sprint?\n4. Are regressions caught and treated as critical?\n5. Is the debt trend improving or worsening over time?\n", "sector": "procedural", "salience": 1.0}, {"id": "skill_1", "label": "accessibility-testing-strategy", "group": "industrial", "content": "---\nname: accessibility-testing-strategy\ndescription: \"Plan what to test, how to test, and who should test for accessibility. The other 70%\nrequire human judgment.\n\n## The Testing Pyramid\n\n### Layer 1: Automated Testing (Catches ~30%)\nWhat it finds:\n- Missing alt text\n- Colour contrast failures\n- Missing form labels\n- Missing language attribute\n- Empty headings and buttons\n- Duplicate IDs\n\nWhat it misses:\n- Whether alt text is meaningful\n- Whether focus order is logical\n- Whether content makes assessment\n- Cognitive accessibility barriers\n- Interaction pattern issues\n\nMethod:\n- Tab through every interactive element\n- Test with screen reader (VoiceOver or NVDA)\n- Test at 200% zoom\n- Test with system preferences enabled\n- Review content for plain language\n\nWhen: before each release, on major feature changes\nWho: someone trained in accessibility testing\n\n### Layer 3: User Testing With Disabled People (Catches remaining ~20%)\nWhat it finds:\n- Real-world usability barriers that experts miss\n- Assistive technology compatibility issues\n- Unexpected workflows and workarounds\n- Cognitive and emotional barriers\n- Issues that only appear with actual AT proficiency\n\nMethod:\n- Recruit participants with diverse disabilities\n- Test core tasks with their own devices and AT\n- Observe ÔÇö don't guide or explain\n- Record what works, what fails, and what frustrates\n\nWhen: quarterly, or before major launches\nWho: users with disabilities using their own setup\n\n## What to Test First\n\n### Priority 1: Core Tasks\n- Can users complete the primary tasks the product exists for?\n- Test the critical path end to end\n- Any failure here is a launch blocker\n\n### Priority 2: Input and Navigation\n- Keyboard-only navigation through the core flow\n- Screen reader navigation through the core flow\n- Touch interaction on mobile for the core flow\n\n### Priority 3: Content and Comprehension\n- Is content understandable at the target reading level?\n- Are error messages helpful?\n- Is help available and findable?\n\n### Priority 4: Edge Cases and Preferences\n- Zoom to 200%\n- System preferences (reduced motion, high contrast, dark mode)\n- Long content, empty states, error states\n\n## Screen Reader Testing Basics\n\nFor teams new J\n- Turn off: Cmd + F5\n\n### Start With NVDA (Windows)\n- Download free from nvaccess.org\n- Navigate: Tab, arrow keys\n- Headings: H\n- Links: K\n- Forms: F\n- Stop speech: Ctrl\n\n### What to Listen For\n- Does every interactive element have a name?\n- Are state changes announced (expanded, selected, checked)?\n- Does focus move to the right place after actions?\n- Can you understand the Audio content needs a visual equivalent.\nVisual content needs an audio or text equivalent.\n\n## Captions\n\n### What Captions Include\n- All spoken dialogue, attributed to the speaker\n- Relevant sound effects: [door slams], [phone rings], [laughter]\n- Music that conveys mood or meaning: [upbeat music], [tense score]\n- Off-screen sounds that matter: [footsteps approaching]\n\n### Caption Quality\n- Synchronised with speech (within 1 second)\n- Minimum 1 second display time, maximum 2 lines\n- No more than 32 characters per line for readability\n- Proper punctuation and grammar ÔÇö not auto-generated without review\n- Speaker identification when multiple speakers are present\n\n### Auto-Captions Are Not Enough\n- Auto-generated captions typically have 80ÔÇô85% accuracy\n- That means 1 in 5 or 6 words is wrong\n- Always review and correct auto-captions\n- Names, technical terms, and accented speech fail most often\n\n## Transcripts\n\n### When to Provide\n- Every audio-only file (podcast, voice memo, audio article)\n- Every video (as a complement to captions)\n- Transcripts serve people who are deaf, hard of hearing, or who\n  prefer reading to watching/listening\n\n### What content (helps navigation)\n\n### Transcript Placement\n- Link directly below or beside the media player\n- Label clearly: \"Read the full transcript\"\n- Don't hide behind multiple clicks\n- Make and seek controls\n- Visible captions toggle\n- Speed controls (0.5x to 2x) ÔÇö essential for cognitive accessibility\n- Volume control independent of system volume\n- No autoplay ÔÇö let the user Is autoplay disabled?\n", "sector": "procedural", "salience": 1.0}, {"id": "skill_4", "label": "responsive-accessibility", "group": "industrial", "content": "---\nname: responsive-accessibility\ndescription: \"Design responsive layouts that maintain accessibility across screen sizes, zoom levels, and orientations. Use when designing responsive interfaces, testing zoom behaviour, or reviewing layouts across devices. Triggers on: responsive, zoom, reflow, breakpoint, mobile accessibility, screen size, orientation, viewport, 200% zoom, text scaling, layout shift, horizontal scroll.\"\n---\n\n# Responsive Accessibility\n\nResponsive design isn't just about screen size ÔÇö it's about ensuring\naccessibility holds at every viewport, zoom level, and orientation.\nA layout that works at 1440px but breaks at 200% zoom has failed\nthe users who need zoom most.\n\n## Core Requirements\n\n### Text Reflow at 200% Zoom\n- All content must reflow into a single column at 200% zoom\n  with no horizontal scrolling\n- This is WCAG 2.1 Level AA (Success Criterion 1.4.10)\n- Test by setting browser zoom to 200% at 1280px viewport ÔÇö\n  the result must be usable without horizontal scroll\n- Exceptions: data tables, maps, and diagrams may scroll\n  horizontally if no information is lost\n\n### Text Scaling to 200%\n- All text must remain readable when scaled to 200%\n- Text must not be clipped, truncated, or overlap other elements\n- Use relative units (rem, em, %) not fixed pixels for text\n- Line height and spacing should scale proportionally\n\n### Orientation Freedom\n- Content must work in both portrait and landscape orientation\n- Never lock orientation unless essential to function\n  (a piano app may need landscape; a form never does)\n- Some users mount devices in fixed orientations due to\n  physical setup ÔÇö locking orientation can block access entirely\n\n## Design Patterns\n\n### Fluid Layout\n- Use flexible grids that adapt rather than fixed breakpoints\n  that snap\n- Set max-width on content containers for readability\n  (45ÔÇô75 characters per line)\n- Let whitespace compress before content reflows\n- Avoid fixed-width elements that force horizontal scroll\n\n### Content Priority\n- Define what's essential at each breakpoint\n- On small screens: show core content, provide access to rest\n- Don't hide critical functionality behind \"desktop only\" features\n- Navigation should adapt (hamburger menu is fine) but all\n  destinations must remain accessible\n\n### Touch and Pointer Adaptation\n- At small viewports: increase touch targets to 48px minimum\n- At large viewports: pointer-friendly sizes are acceptable\n  (24px minimum)\n- Detect pointer type with `@media (pointer: coarse)` for\n  touch-first sizing\n- Spacing between targets should increase on touch devices\n\n### Zoom-Safe Patterns\n- Sticky headers must not consume excessive vertical space at zoom\n  (a header that takes 30% of the viewport at 200% zoom is a barrier)\n- Fixed-position elements must be tested at 200% zoom\n- Tooltips and popovers must remain visible and reachable at zoom\n- Modal dialogs must be scrollable if content exceeds viewport at zoom\n\n## Testing Protocol\n\n1. Test at 100%, 150%, and 200% browser zoom on a 1280px viewport\n2. Test at 320px viewport width (mobile equivalent)\n3. Test in both portrait and landscape orientation\n4. Test with system font size set to largest option\n5. At each test point verify: no horizontal scroll, no clipped text,\n   no overlapping elements, all interactions functional\n\n## Assessment Checklist\n\n- [ ] Content reflows at 200% zoom without horizontal scrolling\n- [ ] Text remains readable at 200% scaling\n- [ ] Both orientations are supported\n- [ ] Touch targets increase at small viewports\n- [ ] Sticky/fixed elements don't dominate the viewport at zoom\n- [ ] No functionality is lost at any supported viewport size\n- [ ] All text uses relative units\n", "sector": "procedural", "salience": 1.0}, {"id": "skill_5", "label": "table-accessibility", "group": "industrial", "content": "---\nname: table-accessibility\ndescription: \"Design data tables that work for screen readers and cognitive accessibility. Use when creating or reviewing tables, data grids, comparison tables, pricing tables, or any tabular data. Triggers on: table, data table, grid, spreadsheet, comparison table, pricing table, screen reader table, table headers, scope, caption, complex table.\"\n---\n\n# Table Accessibility\n\nDesign tables that communicate structure and relationships ÔÇö not just\nto sighted users scanning rows and columns, but to screen reader users\nwho navigate one cell at a time.\n\n## When to Use a Table\n\nTables are for data that has relationships across two dimensions (rows\nand columns). Don't use tables for layout.\n\n- Use a table: comparing features across products, showing schedules,\n  displaying data with consistent categories\n- Don't use a table: laying out a form, arranging cards, creating\n  visual columns of unrelated content\n\n## Core Requirements\n\n### Every Table Needs Headers\n- Column headers: use <th> with scope=\"col\"\n- Row headers: use <th> with scope=\"row\"\n- Screen readers announce headers before each cell, so users always\n  know which column and row they're in\n- Without headers, a screen reader user hears a stream of disconnected\n  values with no context\n\n### Every Table Needs a Caption or Label\n- Use <caption> element for HTML tables\n- Or use aria-labelledby pointing to a visible heading\n- The caption describes what the table contains:\n  \"Pricing comparison for Standard, Pro, and Enterprise plans\"\n- Not just \"Table 1\" or \"Data\"\n\n### Simple Tables Over Complex Tables\n- Prefer simple tables with one row of column headers\n- Avoid merged cells (colspan, rowspan) wherever possible\n- If merged cells are necessary, use proper headers/id/scope\n  associations\n- If a table needs merged cells to make sense, consider whether\n  it should be split into multiple simpler tables\n\n## Cognitive Accessibility for Tables\n\n### Visual Design\n- Zebra striping (alternating row colours) aids row tracking\n- Adequate cell padding (at least 8px) prevents crowding\n- Align numbers right, text left for readability\n- Highlight the header row with stronger contrast\n\n### Reduce Complexity\n- Maximum 5ÔÇô7 columns for comfortable scanning\n- If more columns are needed, consider a different format\n  or allow users to show/hide columns\n- Provide a summary or key takeaway above complex tables\n- Don't require horizontal scrolling ÔÇö make tables responsive\n\n### Responsive Tables\n- On small screens: convert to card/list view where each row\n  becomes a labelled card\n- Or allow horizontal scroll with a sticky first column\n- Never hide data on smaller screens without a way to access it\n- Test: can a mobile user access every piece of data?\n\n## Assessment Checklist\n\n- [ ] All tables have column and/or row headers with proper scope\n- [ ] All tables have a caption or accessible label\n- [ ] No merged cells (or properly associated headers if unavoidable)\n- [ ] Tables are responsive on small screens\n- [ ] Tables are not used for visual layout\n- [ ] Complex tables have a text summary of key findings\n- [ ] Cell padding and spacing support readability\n", "sector": "procedural", Use when planning accessibility coverage, identifying gaps, or communicating accessibility scope to stakeholders. Triggers on: ability spectrum, range of users, who can use this, who can't use this, accessibility coverage, accessibility scope, ability range, spectrum of needs.\"\n---\n\n# Ability Spectrum Mapping\n\nMap how a feature performs across the full range of human ability ÔÇö\nfrom full capability to no capability in each dimension ÔÇö to identify\nwhere the design starts to break down and who gets excluded.\n\n## Why a Spectrum\n\nAccessibility is not binary. It's not \"can see\" vs \"blind.\" The\nreality is a continuous spectrum:\n\n- Full vision ÔåÆ needs glasses ÔåÆ low vision ÔåÆ legally blind ÔåÆ no vision\n- Full hearing ÔåÆ mild loss ÔåÆ moderate loss ÔåÆ severe loss ÔåÆ deaf\n- Full dexterity ÔåÆ mild tremor ÔåÆ limited range ÔåÆ single hand ÔåÆ no hands\n- Full cognition ÔåÆ mild difficulty ÔåÆ moderate difficulty ÔåÆ significant support needs\n\nProducts don't go from \"works\" to \"doesn't work\" in a single step.\nThey degrade gradually. The spectrum map shows you exactly where your\ndesign starts failing and for whom.\n\n## How to Build an Ability Spectrum Map\n\n### Step 1: List Core Tasks\nIdentify the 5ÔÇô10 most important tasks a user performs with the product.\n\n### Step 2: Define the Spectrums\nFor each relevant ability dimension, define 4ÔÇô5 points:\n\n**Vision spectrum:**\nFull vision ÔåÆ Corrected vision ÔåÆ Low vision (magnification) ÔåÆ\nVery low vision (screen reader + some vision) ÔåÆ No vision (screen reader only)\n\n**Hearing spectrum:**\nFull hearing ÔåÆ Mild loss (louder volume) ÔåÆ Moderate loss (captions needed) ÔåÆ\nSevere loss (captions + visual alerts) ÔåÆ Deaf (fully visual communication)\n\n**Motor spectrum:**\nFull dexterity ÔåÆ Reduced precision (larger targets) ÔåÆ Limited range\n(keyboard only) ÔåÆ Minimal movement (switch or voice) ÔåÆ\nNo hand use (eye tracking, sip-and-puff)\n\n**Cognitive spectrum:**\nComfortable with complexity ÔåÆ Prefers simplicity ÔåÆ Needs plain language ÔåÆ\nNeeds step-by-step guidance ÔåÆ Needs significant support\n\n### Step 3: Map Each Task\n\nFor each task ├ù ability point, rate:\n- **Works well** ÔÇö no barriers\n- **Works with friction** ÔÇö possible but harder than it should be\n- **Works with workaround** ÔÇö user must find their own solution\n- **Fails** ÔÇö task cannot be completed\n\n### Step 4: Identify the Breakpoints\n\nWhere does the design shift from \"works\" to \"fails\"? That breakpoint\nis your accessibility boundary. Everything to the right of it is\nexcluded.\n\nThe goal is to push that boundary as far right as possible.\n\n## Presenting to Stakeholders\n\nThe spectrum map is a powerful communication tool because it shows:\n- Exactly how many users are affected at each point\n- Where investment in accessibility has the highest return\n- That accessibility is a continuum, not a checkbox\n- Which fixes would move the boundary and include more people\n\n## Template\n\n| Task | Full vision | Low vision | Screen reader |\n|------|------------|-----------|---------------|\n| Search for a product | Works well | Works with friction (small targets) | Fails (search suggestions not announced) |\n| Complete checkout | Works well | Works well | Works with friction (form errors not associated) |\n\n## Assessment Questions\n\n1. Have you mapped core tasks across at least vision, motor, and\n   cognitive spectrums?\n2. Can you identify the exact breakpoint where each task fails?\n3. Do you know which fixes would include the most additional users?\n4. Can you communicate the spectrum to stakeholders without\n   technical jargon?\n", "sector": "procedural", "salience": 1.0}, {"id": "skill_19", "label": "accessible-content-review", "group": "industrial", "content": "---\nname: review\ndescription: \"Review content for accessibility across cognitive and literacy levels. content could be improved but is functional\n", "sector": "procedural", "salience": 1.0}, {"id": "skill_20", "label": "adaptive-personalisation", "group": "industrial", "content": "---\nname: contextual-help-design\ndescription: \"Design help systems and support patterns that work for people with cognitive disabilities. Use when designing help content, tooltips, onboarding, FAQs, support flows, documentation, or any context where users need guidance. Triggers on: help, support, FAQ, tooltip, onboarding, tutorial, guidance, stuck, confused, documentation, instructions.\"\n---\n\n# Contextual Help Design\n\nDesign help that meets people where they are ÔÇö on the screen they're\nstuck on, in language they understand, without requiring them to\nremember how they got there.\n\n## Core Principle\n\nIf a user gets stuck, they should be able to get help without leaving\nthe screen they're on.\n\n## Design Patterns\n\n### Inline Help\n- Tooltip or info icon next to fields that need explanation\n- Examples shown next to input fields (\"e.g., 15/03/2026\")\n- Help text visible by default for complex fields\n- Inline definitions for specialist terms (underline + tooltip)\n- \"What's this?\" links for uncommon concepts\n\n### Guided Tasks\n- Step-by-step wizard as an alternative to complex forms\n- Examples of completed tasks (filled-in sample forms)\n- Templates or pre-built options alongside \"start from scratch\"\n- Progress-aware hints that change based on where the user is\n\n### Escalation Path\n- Self-service help (search, FAQ) as first option\n- Chat or messaging as second option\n- Phone or video as third option\n- Never require users to re-explain when escalating between channels\n- Show estimated wait times for human support\n- Never require a reference number to get help\n\n### Error Recovery Support\n- Error messages include: what happened + why + what to do next\n- Link directly to the field or step that needs fixing\n- Offer alternative paths when primary fails\n- For complex errors: offer human support directly\n\n## Help Content Rules\n\n- Write at grade 4ÔÇô6 reading level\n- Use the same terminology as the interface (not different words)\n- One instruction per step\n- Include images or diagrams for spatial or procedural instructions\n- Make help searchable\n- Test help content with people who have the actual problem\n\n## Assessment Questions\n\n1. data: Optional[T] = None\n    error: Optional[str] = None\n\n    @classmethod\n    def ok(cls, data: T) -> \"ApiResponse[T]\":\n        return cls(success=True, data=data)\n\n    @classmethod\n    def Custom Hooks (React)\n\n```typescript\nimport { useState, useCallback } from 'react'\n\ninterface UseApiState<T> {\n  data: T | null\n  loading: boolean\n  error: string | null\n}\n\nexport function useApi<T>(\n  fetchFn: () => Promise<ApiResponse<T>>\n) {\n  const [state, setState] = useState<UseApiState<T>>({\n    data: null,\n    loading: false,\n    error: null,\n  })\n\n  const execute = useCallback(async () => {\n    setState(prev => ({ ...prev, loading: true, error: null }))\n\n    const result = await fetchFn()\n\n    if (result.success) {\n      setState({ data: result.data!, loading: false, error: null })\n    } else {\n      setState({ data: null, loading: false, error: result.error! })\n    }\n  }, [fetchFn])\n\n  return { ...state, execute }\n}\n```\n\n---\n\n## This skill covers tool design from schema to error handling. JSON Schema best practices, description writing that actually helps the LLM, validation, and the emerging MCP standard that's becoming the lingua franca for AI tools.  Key insight: Tool descriptions are more important than tool implementa\"\nsource: Jack Industrial Registry\n---\n\n# Agent Tool Builder\n\nYou are an expert in the interface between LLMs and the outside world.\nYou've seen tools that work beautifully and tools that cause agents to\nhallucinate, loop, or fail silently. The difference is almost always\nin the design, not the implementation.\n\nYour core insight: The LLM never sees your code. It only sees the schema\nand description. A perfectly implemented tool with a vague description\nwill fail. A simple tool with crystal-clear documentation will succeed.\n\nYou push for explicit error hand\n\n## Capabilities\n\n- agent-tools\n- function-calling\n- tool-schema-design\n- mcp-tools\n- tool-validation\n- tool-error-handling\n\n## Patterns\n\n## ­ƒºá Knowledge Modules (Fractal Skills)\n\n### 1. [ÔØî Too Many Tools](./sub-skills/too-many-tools.md)\n", "sector": "procedural", "salience": 1.0}, {"id": "skill_36", "label": "sub-skills", "group": "industrial", "content": "# ÔØî Silent Failures\n\n", "sector": "procedural", "salience": 1.0}, {"id": "skill_37", "label": "sub-skills", "group": "industrial", "content": "# ÔØî Too Many Tools\n\n## Related Skills\n\nWorks well with: `multi-agent-orchestration`, `api-designer`, `llm-architect`, `backend`", "sector": "procedural", "salience": 1.0}, {"id": "skill_38", "label": "sub-skills", "group": "industrial", "content": "# Tool Error Handling\n\nReturning errors that help the LLM recover\n\n## Anti-Patterns", "sector": "procedural", "salience": 1.0}, {"id": "skill_39", "label": "sub-skills", "group": "industrial", "content": "# Tool Schema Design\n\nCreating clear, unambiguous JSON Schema for tools", "sector": "procedural", "salience": 1.0}, {"id": "skill_40", "label": "sub-skills", "group": "industrial", "content": "# Tool with Input Examples\n\nUsing examples to guide LLM tool usage", "sector": "procedural", "salience": 1.0}, {"id": "skill_41", "label": "sub-skills", "group": "industrial", "content": "# ÔØî Vague Descriptions\n\n", "sector": "procedural", "salience": 1.0}, {"id": "skill_42", "label": "alt-text-design", "group": "industrial", "content": "---\nname: alt-text-design\ndescription: \"Write meaningful alternative text for images, charts, diagrams, and visual content. Use when creating or reviewing alt text, image descriptions, chart summaries, or any visual content that needs a text equivalent. Triggers on: alt text, image description, alternative text, screen reader, decorative image, chart description, infographic, data visualisation, complex image, meaningful alt.\"\n---\n\n# Alt Text Design\n\nWrite alt text that conveys the same information and purpose as the\nvisual content ÔÇö not just what the image looks like, but what it\ncommunicates.\n\n## Core Principle\n\nAlt text answers the question: if this image disappeared, what\ninformation would be lost? That's what needs to be in the alt text.\n\n## Decision Tree\n\n### 1. Is the image purely decorative?\n- Background textures, dividers, visual flourishes\n- Images that repeat information already in adjacent text\n- **Action:** use empty alt (alt=\"\") so screen readers skip it\n\n### 2. Is the image functional?\n- A linked logo, an icon button, an image link\n- **Action:** alt text describes the function, not the image\n- Logo linking to homepage: alt=\"Company name ÔÇö home\"\n- Search icon button: alt=\"Search\"\n- NOT alt=\"magnifying glass icon\"\n\n### 3. Is the image informative?\n- Photos, illustrations, screenshots that add information\n- **Action:** describe the information the image conveys\n- Photo of a damaged product: alt=\"Crack running along the\n  bottom edge of the screen\"\n- NOT alt=\"Photo of a phone\" or alt=\"product-image-032.jpg\"\n\n### 4. Is the image complex?\n- Charts, graphs, diagrams, infographics, maps\n- **Action:** short alt text summarising the key message PLUS a\n  longer description nearby (in the page text or a linked detail)\n- Bar chart: alt=\"Bar chart showing sales growth ÔÇö details below\"\n  followed by a text summary of the data\n- NOT alt=\"bar chart\" and not the entire data set in the alt attribute\n\n## Writing Good Alt Text\n\n### Be Specific\n- Bad: \"A group of people\"\n- Good: \"Three team members reviewing a prototype on a whiteboard\"\n\n### Convey Purpose, Not Just Appearance\n- Bad: \"Red circle with a number\"\n- Good: \"3 unread notifications\"\n\n### Match the Context\nThe same image needs different alt text in different contexts:\n- On a recipe page: \"Finished risotto in a white bowl, garnished\n  with parmesan and basil\"\n- On a kitchenware product page: \"Round white ceramic serving bowl,\n  22cm diameter\"\n\n### Keep It Concise\n- Aim for one sentence, two at most\n- Don't start with \"Image of...\" or \"Picture of...\" ÔÇö screen readers\n  already announce it as an image\n- For complex images: keep alt text short and provide detail elsewhere\n\n## Specific Content Types\n\n### Charts and Graphs\n- Alt text: state the type and the key takeaway\n- Provide a text summary or data table nearby\n- \"Line chart showing website traffic doubling between January and\n  June 2026. Full data in the table below.\"\n\n### Screenshots\n- Describe what the screenshot shows that matters to the reader\n- \"Settings panel with the notifications toggle switched off\"\n- NOT \"Screenshot of the settings page\"\n\n### Icons with Labels\n- If the icon has a visible text label, the icon is decorative (alt=\"\")\n- If the icon is the only indicator, the alt text must convey the meaning\n\n### Images of Text\n- Avoid images of text wherever possible ÔÇö use real text\n- If unavoidable: the alt text must contain the full text in the image\n\n## Assessment Questions\n\n1. Does every non-decorative image have meaningful alt text?\n2. Does the alt text convey purpose, not just appearance?\n3. Are decorative images marked with empty alt?\n4. Do complex images have both short alt text and a longer description?\n5. Would someone using a screen reader get the same information as\n   someone seeing the image?\n", "sector": "procedural", "salience": 1.0}, {"id": "skill_43", "label": "api-design-principles", "group": "industrial", "content": "---\nversion: 4.1.0-fractal\nname: api-design-principles\ndescription: Master REST and GraphQL API design principles to build intuitive, scalable, and maintainable APIs that delight developers. Checklist\n\n## Pre-Implementation Review\n\n### Resource Design\n\n- [ ] Resources are nouns, not verbs\n- [ ] Plural names for collections\n- [ ] Consistent naming across all endpoints\n- [ ] Clear resource hierarchy (avoid deep nesting >2 levels)\n- [ ] All CRUD operations properly mapped to HTTP methods\n\n### HTTP Methods\n\n- [ ] GET for retrieval (safe, idempotent)\n- [ ] POST for creation\n- [ ] PUT for full replacement (idempotent)\n- [ ] PATCH for partial updates\n- [ ] DELETE for removal (idempotent)\n\n### Status Codes\n\n- [ ] 200 OK for successful GET/PATCH/PUT\n- [ ] 201 Created for POST\n- [ ] 204 No Content for DELETE\n- [ ] 400 Bad Request for malformed requests\n- [ ] 401 Unauthorized for missing auth\n- [ ] 403 Forbidden for insufficient permissions\n- [ ] 404 Not Found for missing resources\n- [ ] 422 Unprocessable Entity for validation errors\n- [ ] 429 Too Many Requests for rate limiting\n- [ ] 500 Internal Server Error for server issues\n\n### Pagination\n\n- [ ] All collection endpoints paginated\n- [ ] Default page size defined (e.g., 20)\n- [ ] Maximum page size enforced (e.g., 100)\n- [ ] Pagination metadata included (total, pages, etc.)\n- [ ] Cursor-based or offset-based pattern chosen\n\n### Filtering & Sorting\n\n- [ ] Query parameters for filtering\n- [ ] Sort parameter supported\n- [ ] Search parameter for full-text in error responses\n\n### Authentication & Authorization\n\n- [ ] Authentication method defined (Bearer token, API key)\n- [ ] Authorization checks on all endpoints\n- [ ] 401 vs 403 used correctly\n- provided\n\n### Documentation\n\n- [ ] OpenAPI/Swagger spec generated\n- [ ] All endpoints documented\n- [ ] Request/response examples provided\n- [ ] Error responses documented\n- [ ] Authentication flow documented\n\n### Testing\n\n- [ ] Unit tests for business logic\n- [ ] Integration tests for endpoints\n- [ ] Error scenarios tested\n- [ ] Edge cases covered\n- [ ] Performance tests for heavy
